Analyzing the game state with 21 items and the possibility of taking 1, 2, or 3 items, we must consider the concept of the nim-sum and its implications in Nim-Misere.  Unlike standard Nim, where a nim-sum of 0 indicates a losing position, in Nim-Misere, a nim-sum of 0 for the *opponent* is the winning strategy.  Let's analyze the nim-sums resulting from each possible move:

* Taking 1 item leaves 20 items. The binary representation of 20 is 10100. The nim-sum is 1. 
* Taking 2 items leaves 19 items. The binary representation of 19 is 10011. The nim-sum is 2. 
* Taking 3 items leaves 18 items. The binary representation of 18 is 10010. The nim-sum is 1.

None of these moves directly leave the opponent with a nim-sum of 0.  However, the goal is to force the opponent into a losing position.  A common strategy in Nim-Misere is to leave the opponent with a number of items that is a multiple of 4.  This is because any move the opponent makes will leave a non-multiple of 4, and this pattern can be maintained until the opponent is forced to take the last item. By taking 2 items, we leave 19 items.  Regardless of the opponent's move (1, 2, or 3 items), I can always respond in a way to leave a multiple of 4. This pattern guarantees a win.  Therefore, the optimal move is to take 2 items.

Taking 3 items leaves 22 items. Again, we cannot guarantee a win by simply following a pattern of leaving multiples of 4 minus 1.

The key is to force the opponent into a position where they *must* leave you with one item.  The 4k+1 pattern is useful but not sufficient to guarantee a win in Nim-Misere.  A more robust approach is to analyze the game state recursively from the end.  Let's consider the endgame:

* 1 item: Losing position
* 2 items: Winning position (take 1)
* 3 items: Winning position (take 2)
* 4 items: Winning position (take 3)
* 5 items: Winning position (take 4)

And so on.  If we leave the opponent with 1 item, they lose.  Consider the sequence of moves:

1. We take 3 items, leaving 22.
2. The opponent takes x items (1,2, or 3).
3. We take 4-x items, leaving a multiple of 4 minus 1 items.
4. We continue this pattern until we leave the opponent with 1 item.

This strategy guarantees a win.  Therefore, taking 3 items is the optimal and most reliable move.
